          - paragraph
            - StaticText "Apollo 11"
            - StaticText " (July 16–24, 1969) was the American "
            - link "spaceflight" [ref=e84]
            - StaticText " that first landed "
            - link "humans" [ref=e85]
            - StaticText " on the "
            - link "Moon" [ref=e86]
            - StaticText ", and the fifth "
            - link "crewed mission" [ref=e87]
            - StaticText " of "
            - link "NASA" [ref=e88]
            - StaticText "'s "
            - link "Apollo program" [ref=e89]
            - StaticText ". The mission was crewed by Commander "
            - link "Neil Armstrong" [ref=e90]
            - StaticText ", Command Module Pilot "
            - link "Michael Collins" [ref=e91]
            - StaticText ", and Lunar Module Pilot "
            - link "Edwin \"Buzz\" Aldrin" [ref=e92]
            - StaticText ", all of whom were on their second and final spaceflight."
          - paragraph
            - StaticText "Launched atop a "
            - link "Saturn V" [ref=e93]
            - StaticText " rocket from "
            - link "Kennedy Space Center" [ref=e94]
            - StaticText " in "
            - link "Florida" [ref=e95]
            - StaticText " on July 16 at 13:32 "
            - link "UTC" [ref=e96]
            - StaticText ", the Apollo spacecraft consisted of three parts: the "
            - link "command module" [ref=e97]
            - StaticText " (CM), which housed the three astronauts and was the only part to return to Earth; the "
            - link "service module" [ref=e98]
            - StaticText " (SM), which provided propulsion, electrical power, oxygen, and water to the command module; and the "
            - link "Lunar Module" [ref=e99]
            - StaticText " (LM), which had two stages—a descent stage with a large engine and fuel tanks for landing on the Moon, and a lighter ascent stage containing a cabin for two astronauts and a small engine to return them to "
            - link "lunar orbit" [ref=e100]
            - StaticText ". After a three-day transit, Armstrong and Aldrin descended to the surface aboard the LM "
            - link "Eagle" [ref=e354]
            - StaticText ", landing in the Sea of Tranquility ("
            - link "Mare Tranquillitatis" [ref=e101]
            - StaticText ") on July 20 at 20:17 UTC while Collins remained in lunar orbit aboard the CM "
            - link "Columbia" [ref=e355]
            - StaticText ". Armstrong became the first human to walk on the Moon approximately six hours after landing, followed by Aldrin nineteen minutes later. Together they spent around two and a half hours walking on the surface, planting an "
            - link "American flag" [ref=e102]
            - StaticText ", speaking with President "
            - link "Richard Nixon" [ref=e103]
            - StaticText " who was in the "
            - link "White House" [ref=e104]
            - StaticText ", deploying scientific instruments, and collecting 21.5"
            - StaticText "kg (47.5"
            - StaticText "lb) of lunar material. After more than 21 hours on the surface, they rejoined Collins in lunar orbit, and the crew returned safely to "
            - link "Earth" [ref=e105]
            - StaticText " on July 24, splashing down in the "
            - link "Pacific Ocean" [ref=e106]
            - StaticText "."
          - paragraph
            - StaticText "Apollo 11 was the culmination of the "
            - link "Space Race" [ref=e107]
            - StaticText ", a geopolitical competition between the United States and the "
            - link "Soviet Union" [ref=e108]
            - StaticText " rooted in "
            - link "Cold War" [ref=e109]
            - StaticText " rivalry. It fulfilled a national goal set by President "
            - link "John F. Kennedy" [ref=e110]
            - StaticText " in May 1961, who had "
            - link "challenged" [ref=e111]
            - StaticText " the United States to land a man on the Moon and return him safely before the end of the decade. The program overcame a severe setback with the fatal "
            - link "Apollo 1" [ref=e112]
            - StaticText " launchpad fire in January 1967 and drew on technologies developed through the preceding "
            - link "Mercury" [ref=e113]
            - StaticText " and "
            - link "Gemini" [ref=e114]
            - StaticText " programs. The Soviet Union, despite early leads in spaceflight, was unable to match the Saturn V rocket, and its uncrewed lunar probe "
            - link "Luna 15" [ref=e115]
            - StaticText " crashed on the Moon on July 21, while Neil Armstrong and Buzz Aldrin were still on the lunar surface."
          - paragraph
            - StaticText "Armstrong's moonwalk was broadcast live to an estimated 600 million viewers, roughly one-fifth of the world's population, making it among the most-watched "
            - link "television" [ref=e116]
            - StaticText " events in history. Stepping onto the lunar surface, Armstrong declared: \""
            - link "That's one small step for [a] man, one giant leap for mankind" [ref=e117]
            - StaticText ".\" "
            - link "Lunar samples" [ref=e118]
            - StaticText " returned by the mission led to the identification of three previously unrecognized minerals, and scientific instruments deployed on the surface continued returning data for years afterwards. The crew were honoured with "
            - link "ticker-tape parades" [ref=e119]
            - StaticText ", a world goodwill tour, and the "
            - link "Presidential Medal of Freedom" [ref=e120]
            - StaticText ". The command module "
            - StaticText "Columbia"
            - StaticText " is preserved at the "
            - link "National Air and Space Museum" [ref=e121]
            - StaticText " in "
            - link "Washington, D.C." [ref=e122]
            - StaticText ", while the descent stage of "
            - StaticText "Eagle"
            - StaticText " remains on the lunar surface."
        - region "Background" [ref=e14]

          - paragraph

            - StaticText " On October 4, 1957, the Soviet Union launched "

            - StaticText ", the first "
            - link "artificial satellite" [ref=e126]
            - StaticText ", surprising the world and fueling fears about Soviet technological and military capabilities. Its success demonstrated that the USSR could potentially deliver "
            - link "nuclear weapons" [ref=e127]
            - StaticText " over "
            - link "intercontinental distances" [ref=e128]
            - StaticText ", challenging American claims of military, economic, and technological superiority."

          - paragraph
            - StaticText "Because the Soviet Union had "
            - link "launch vehicles" [ref=e137]
            - StaticText " with higher lift capacity, President John F. Kennedy, Eisenhower's successor, chose a challenge that exceeded the capabilities of the existing generation of rockets, so that both countries would be starting from an equal position. A crewed mission to the Moon would serve this purpose."

          - paragraph
            - StaticText "On May 25, 1961, Kennedy addressed the "
            - link "United States Congress" [ref=e138]
            - StaticText " on \"Urgent National Needs\" and declared:"
          - blockquote
            - paragraph
              - StaticText "I believe that this nation should commit itself to achieving the goal, before this decade [1960s] is out, of landing a man on the Moon and returning him safely to the Earth. No single space project in this period will be more impressive to mankind, or more important for the long-range exploration of space; and none will be so difficult or expensive to accomplish. We propose to accelerate the development of the appropriate lunar space craft. We propose to develop alternate liquid and solid fuel boosters, much larger than any now being developed, until certain which is superior. We propose additional funds for other engine development and for unmanned explorations—explorations which are particularly important for one purpose which this nation will never overlook: the survival of the man who first makes this daring flight. But in a very real sense, it will not be one man going to the Moon—if we make this judgment affirmatively, it will be an entire nation. For all of us must work to put him there."

          - blockquote

              - StaticText "There is no strife, no prejudice, no national conflict in outer space as yet. Its hazards are hostile to us all. Its conquest deserves the best of all mankind, and its opportunity for peaceful cooperation may never come again. But why, some say, the Moon? Why choose this as our goal? And they may well ask, why climb the highest mountain? Why, 35 years ago, "
              - link "fly the Atlantic" [ref=e368]
              - StaticText "? Why does "
              - link "Rice play Texas" [ref=e369]

We choose to go to the Moon in this decade and do the other things, not because they are easy, but because they are hard; because that goal will serve to organize and measure the best of our energies and skills, because that challenge is one that we are willing to accept, one we are unwilling to postpone, and one we intend to win, and the others, too."

            - StaticText ". A "

            - StaticText " is an "

            - StaticText " in which two spacecraft navigate through space and meet up. In July 1962, NASA head "
            - link "James Webb" [ref=e155]

            - paragraph
              - StaticText "The Apollo 11 prime crew had none of the close cheerful camaraderie characterized by that of "
              - link "Apollo 12" [ref=e405]
              - StaticText ". Instead, they forged an amiable working relationship. Armstrong in particular was notoriously aloof, but Collins, who considered himself a loner, confessed to rebuffing Aldrin's attempts to create a more personal relationship."
              - superscript
                - link "[54]" [ref=e849]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "54"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " Aldrin and Collins described the crew as \"amiable strangers\"."
              - superscript
                - link "[55]" [ref=e850]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "55"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " Armstrong did not agree with the assessment, and said \"all the crews I was on worked very well together.\""
              - superscript
                - link "[55]" [ref=e851]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "55"

          - region "Backup crew" [ref=e47]

            - table
              - row
                - columnheader "Position" [ref=e852]
                  - link "Position" [ref=e1258]
                - columnheader "Astronaut" [ref=e853]

            - paragraph
              - StaticText "The backup crew consisted of Lovell as Commander, "
              - link "William Anders" [ref=e407]
              - StaticText " as CMP, and Haise as LMP. Anders had flown with Lovell on Apollo"
              - StaticText "8."
              - superscript
                - link "[51]" [ref=e860]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "51"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " In early 1969, Anders accepted a job with the "
              - link "National Aeronautics and Space Council" [ref=e408]
              - StaticText " effective August 1969, and announced he would retire as an astronaut at that time. "
              - link "Ken Mattingly" [ref=e409]
              - StaticText " was moved from the support crew into parallel training with Anders as backup CMP in case Apollo 11 was delayed past its intended July launch date, at which point Anders would be unavailable."
              - superscript
                - link "[56]" [ref=e861]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "56"
                    - StaticText "]"
            - paragraph
              - StaticText "Under the normal Apollo crew rotation, Lovell, Mattingly, and Haise would have been assigned to "
              - link "Apollo 14" [ref=e410]
              - StaticText ". However, because "
              - link "Alan Shepard" [ref=e411]
              - StaticText " had only recently returned to flight status and needed additional training, NASA reassigned his crew to Apollo 14 and moved Lovell's crew up to fly "
              - link "Apollo 13" [ref=e412]
              - StaticText " instead."
              - superscript
                - link "[a]" [ref=e862]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "a"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " Shortly before launch, Mattingly was replaced by "
              - link "Jack Swigert" [ref=e413]
              - StaticText " due to concerns over possible measles exposure."

          - region "Support crew" [ref=e48]
            - heading "Support crew" [level=3, ref=e414]
            - paragraph
              - StaticText "During Projects Mercury and Gemini, each mission had a prime and a backup crew. For Apollo, NASA added a third group, the support crew. Their job was to maintain the flight plan, checklists, and mission rules, and to keep the prime and backup crews updated on any changes. They also developed procedures—especially for emergencies, so that by the time the prime and backup crews entered the simulators, they could focus on practicing and mastering them."
              - superscript
                - link "[58]" [ref=e864]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "58"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " The support crew for Apollo 11 was Mattingly, "
              - link "Ronald Evans" [ref=e415]
              - StaticText ", and "
              - link "Bill Pogue" [ref=e416]
              - StaticText "."
              - superscript
                - link "[59]" [ref=e865]
                  - generic

                    - StaticText "59"

        - region "Preparations" [ref=e16]
          - heading "Preparations" [level=2, ref=e180]
          - region "Insignia" [ref=e52]
            - heading "Insignia" [level=3, ref=e431]
            - figure
              - link [ref=e432]
                - image
              - Figcaption
                - StaticText "The Apollo 11 insignia. The eagle is carrying an olive branch to represent a peaceful mission"
            - paragraph
              - StaticText "The Apollo 11 "
              - link "mission emblem" [ref=e433]
              - StaticText " was designed by Collins, who wanted a symbol for \"peaceful lunar landing by the United States\". At Lovell's suggestion, he chose the "
              - link "bald eagle" [ref=e434]
              - StaticText ", the "
              - link "national bird" [ref=e435]
              - StaticText " of the United States, as the symbol. Tom Wilson, a simulator instructor, suggested an "
              - link "olive branch" [ref=e436]
              - StaticText " in its beak to represent their peaceful mission. Collins added a lunar background with the Earth in the distance. The sunlight in the image was coming from the wrong direction; the shadow should have been in the lower part of the Earth instead of the left."
              - superscript
                - link "[68]" [ref=e876]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "68"
                    - StaticText "]"
            - paragraph
              - StaticText "Aldrin, Armstrong and Collins decided the Eagle and the Moon would be in their natural colors, and decided on a blue and gold border. Armstrong was concerned that \"eleven\" would not be understood by non-English speakers, so they went with \"Apollo 11\","
              - superscript
                - link "[68]" [ref=e877]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "68"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " and they decided not to put their names on the patch, so it would \"be representative of "
              - StaticText "everyone"
              - StaticText " who had worked toward a lunar landing\"."
              - superscript
                - link "[69]" [ref=e878]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "69"
                    - StaticText "]"
            - paragraph
              - StaticText "An illustrator at the "
              - link "Manned Spacecraft Center" [ref=e437]
              - StaticText " (MSC) did the artwork, which was then sent off to NASA officials for approval."
              - superscript
                - link "[68]" [ref=e879]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "68"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " The design was rejected. "
              - link "Bob Gilruth" [ref=e438]
              - StaticText ", the director of the MSC felt the talons of the eagle looked \"too warlike\"."
              - superscript
                - link "[70]" [ref=e880]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "70"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " After some discussion, the olive branch was moved to the talons."
              - superscript
                - link "[70]" [ref=e881]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "70"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " When the "
              - link "Eisenhower dollar coin" [ref=e439]
              - StaticText " was released in 1971, the patch design provided the eagle for its reverse side."
              - superscript
                - link "[71]" [ref=e882]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "71"
                    - StaticText "]"

              - StaticText "kg) PPKs were carried on Apollo 11: three (one for each astronaut) were stowed on "
              - StaticText "Columbia"

          - region "Site selection" [ref=e55]
            - heading "Site selection" [level=3, ref=e453]
            - generic
              - link "A map of the Moon showing prospective landing sites for Apollo 11. Site 2 in the Sea of Tranquility was chosen." [ref=e900]
                - image "A map of the Moon showing prospective landing sites for Apollo 11. Site 2 in the Sea of Tranquility was chosen."
              - generic
                - link "Mare Tranquillitatis" [ref=e1421]
                  - image
              - generic
                - link "Mare Tranquillitatis" [ref=e1422]
                  - image
              - generic
                - link "Sinus Medii" [ref=e1423]
                  - image
              - generic
                - link "Oceanus Procellarum" [ref=e1424]
                  - image
              - generic
                - link "Oceanus Procellarum" [ref=e1425]
                  - image
            - generic
              - StaticText "A map of the Moon showing prospective landing sites for Apollo 11. Site 2 in the "
              - link "Sea of Tranquility" [ref=e901]
              - StaticText " was chosen."
            - paragraph
              - StaticText "NASA's Apollo Site Selection Board announced five potential landing sites on February 8, 1968. These were the result of two years' worth of studies based on high-resolution photography of the lunar surface by the five uncrewed probes of the "
              - link "Lunar Orbiter program" [ref=e454]
              - StaticText " and information about surface conditions provided by the "
              - link "Surveyor program" [ref=e455]
              - StaticText "."
              - superscript
                - link "[81]" [ref=e902]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "81"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " The best Earth-bound telescopes could not resolve features with the resolution Project Apollo required."
              - superscript
                - link "[82]" [ref=e903]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "82"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " The landing site had to be close to the lunar equator to minimize the amount of propellant required, clear of obstacles to minimize maneuvering, and flat to simplify the task of the landing radar. Scientific value was not a consideration."
              - superscript
                - link "[83]" [ref=e904]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "83"
                    - StaticText "]"
            - paragraph
              - StaticText "Areas that appeared promising on photographs taken on Earth were often found to be totally unacceptable. The original requirement that the site be free of craters had to be relaxed, as no such site was found."
              - superscript
                - link "[84]" [ref=e905]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "84"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " Five sites were considered: Sites"
              - StaticText "1 and"
              - StaticText "2 were in the Sea of Tranquility ("
              - link "Mare Tranquillitatis" [ref=e906]
              - StaticText "); Site"
              - StaticText "3 was in the Central Bay ("
              - link "Sinus Medii" [ref=e907]
              - StaticText "); and Sites"
              - StaticText "4 and"
              - StaticText "5 were in the Ocean of Storms ("
              - link "Oceanus Procellarum" [ref=e908]
              - StaticText ")."
              - superscript
                - link "[81]" [ref=e909]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "81"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " The final site selection was based on seven criteria:"
            - list
              - listitem [level=1]
                - ListMarker "• "
                - StaticText "The site needed to be smooth, with relatively few craters;"
              - listitem [level=1]
                - ListMarker "• "
                - StaticText "with approach paths free of large hills, tall cliffs or deep craters that might confuse the landing radar and cause it to issue incorrect readings;"
              - listitem [level=1]
                - ListMarker "• "
                - StaticText "reachable with a minimum amount of propellant;"
              - listitem [level=1]
                - ListMarker "• "
                - StaticText "allowing for delays in the launch countdown;"
              - listitem [level=1]
                - ListMarker "• "
                - StaticText "providing the Apollo spacecraft with a free-return trajectory, one that would allow it to coast around the Moon and safely return to Earth without requiring any engine firings should a problem arise on the way to the Moon;"
              - listitem [level=1]
                - ListMarker "• "
                - StaticText "with good visibility during the landing approach, meaning the Sun would be between 7"
                - StaticText "and 20 degrees behind the LM; and"
              - listitem [level=1]
                - ListMarker "• "
                - StaticText "a general slope of less than two degrees in the landing area."
                - superscript
                  - link "[81]" [ref=e1312]
                    - generic
                      - StaticText "["
                      - StaticText "81"
                      - StaticText "]"
            - paragraph
              - StaticText "The requirement for the Sun angle was particularly restrictive, limiting the launch date to one day per month."
              - superscript
                - link "[81]" [ref=e910]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "81"
                    - StaticText "]"

              - StaticText "One of the first versions of the egress checklist had the lunar module pilot exit the spacecraft before the commander, which matched what had been done on Gemini missions,"

              - StaticText " Reporters wrote in early 1969 that Aldrin would be the first man to walk on the Moon, and Associate Administrator "
              - link "George Mueller" [ref=e457]
              - StaticText " told reporters he would be first as well. Aldrin heard that Armstrong would be the first because Armstrong was a civilian, which made Aldrin livid. Aldrin attempted to persuade other Lunar Module pilots he should be first, but they responded cynically about what they perceived as a lobbying campaign. Attempting to stem interdepartmental conflict, Slayton told Aldrin that Armstrong would be first since he was the commander. The decision was announced in a press conference on April 14, 1969."
              - superscript
                - link "[92]" [ref=e919]

            - paragraph
              - StaticText "For decades, Aldrin believed the final decision was largely driven by the Lunar Module's hatch location. Because the astronauts had their spacesuits on and the spacecraft was so small, maneuvering to exit the spacecraft was difficult. The crew tried a simulation in which Aldrin left the spacecraft first, but he damaged the simulator while attempting to egress. While this was enough for mission planners to make their decision, Aldrin and Armstrong were left in the dark on the decision until late spring."

              - StaticText "Five minutes into the descent burn, and "
              - StaticText "6,000 feet (1,800"
              - StaticText "m) above the surface of the Moon, the "
              - link "LM guidance computer" [ref=e511]
              - StaticText " (LGC) distracted the crew with the first of several unexpected 1201 and 1202 program alarms. Inside Mission Control Center, computer engineer "
              - link "Jack Garman" [ref=e512]
              - StaticText " told "
              - link "Guidance Officer" [ref=e513]
              - StaticText " "
              - link "Steve Bales" [ref=e514]
              - StaticText " it was safe to continue the descent, and this was relayed to the crew. The program alarms indicated \"executive overflows\", meaning the guidance computer could not complete all its tasks in real-time and had to postpone some of them."

            - figure
              - link [ref=e518]
                - image
              - Figcaption
                - link "Eagle" [ref=e966]
                  - StaticText "Eagle"
                - StaticText " in "
                - link "lunar orbit" [ref=e967]
                - StaticText " photographed from "
                - link "Columbia" [ref=e968]
                  - StaticText "Columbia"
            - blockquote
              - paragraph
                - StaticText "To blame the computer for the Apollo 11 problems is like blaming the person who spots a fire and calls the fire department. Actually, the computer was programmed to do more than recognize error conditions. A complete set of recovery programs was incorporated into the software. The software's action, in this case, was to eliminate lower priority tasks and re-establish the more important ones. The computer, rather than almost forcing an abort, prevented an abort. If the computer hadn't recognized this problem and taken recovery action, I doubt if Apollo 11 would have been the successful Moon landing it was."
                - superscript
                  - link "[119]" [ref=e1313]
                    - generic
                      - StaticText "["
                      - StaticText "119"
                      - StaticText "]"
            - paragraph
              - StaticText "During the mission, the cause was diagnosed as the rendezvous radar switch being in the wrong position, causing the computer to process data from both the rendezvous and landing radars at the same time."
              - superscript
                - link "[120]" [ref=e969]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "120"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - superscript
                - link "[121]" [ref=e970]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "121"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " Software engineer "
              - link "Don Eyles" [ref=e519]
              - StaticText " concluded in a 2005 Guidance and Control Conference paper that the problem was due to a hardware design bug previously seen during testing of the first uncrewed LM in "
              - link "Apollo 5" [ref=e520]
              - StaticText ". Having the rendezvous radar on (so it was warmed up in case of an emergency landing abort) should have been irrelevant to the computer, but an electrical phasing mismatch between two parts of the rendezvous radar system could cause the stationary antenna to appear to the computer as dithering back and forth between two positions, depending upon how the hardware randomly powered up. The extra spurious "
              - link "cycle stealing" [ref=e521]
              - StaticText ", as the rendezvous radar updated an involuntary counter, caused the computer alarms."
              - superscript
                - link "[122]" [ref=e971]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "122"
                    - StaticText "]"
          - region "Landing" [ref=e60]

                - Video "Unable to play media." [disabled]

            - paragraph
              - StaticText "Armstrong intended to say \"That's one small step for a man\", but the word \"a\" is not audible in the transmission, and thus was not initially reported by most observers of the live broadcast. When later asked about his quote, Armstrong said he believed he said \"for a man\", and subsequent printed versions of the quote included the \"a\" in square brackets. One explanation for the absence may be that his accent caused him to slur the words \"for a\" together; another is the intermittent nature of the audio and video links to Earth, partly because of storms near Parkes Observatory. A more recent digital analysis of the tape claims to reveal the \"a\" may have been spoken but obscured by static. Other analysis points to the claims of static and slurring as \"face-saving fabrication\", and that Armstrong himself later admitted to misspeaking the line."
              - superscript
                - link "[148]" [ref=e1014]

                - StaticText "Nixon: Hello, Neil and Buzz. I'm talking to you by telephone from the Oval Room at the White House. And this certainly has to be the most historic telephone call ever made from the White House. I just can't tell you how proud we all are of what you have done. For every American, this has to be the proudest day of our lives. And for people all over the world, I am sure that they too join with Americans in recognizing what an immense feat this is. Because of what you have done, the heavens have become a part of man's world. And as you talk to us from the Sea of Tranquility, it inspires us to redouble our efforts to bring peace and tranquility to Earth. For one priceless moment in the whole history of man, all the people on this Earth are truly one: one in their pride in what you have done, and one in our prayers that you will return safely to Earth."

                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "194"
                    - StaticText "]"
            - paragraph
              - StaticText "This altered the flight plan. A different sequence of computer programs was used, one never before attempted. In a conventional entry, trajectory event P64 was followed by P67. For a skip-out re-entry, P65 and P66 were employed to handle the exit and entry parts of the skip. In this case, because they were extending the re-entry but not actually skipping out, P66 was not invoked and instead, P65 led directly to P67. The crew were also warned they would not be in a full-lift (heads-down) attitude when they entered P67."
              - superscript
                - link "[193]" [ref=e1087]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "193"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " The first program's acceleration subjected the astronauts to "

              - StaticText " While Apollo 11 sparked the interest of the world, the follow-on Apollo missions did not hold the interest of the nation."
              - superscript
                - link "[227]" [ref=e1130]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "227"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " One possible explanation was the shift in complexity. Landing someone on the Moon was an easy goal to understand; lunar geology was too abstract for the average person. Another is that Kennedy's goal of landing humans on the Moon had already been accomplished."
              - superscript
                - link "[234]" [ref=e1131]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "234"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " A well-defined objective helped Project Apollo accomplish its goal, but after it was completed it was hard to justify continuing the lunar missions."
              - superscript
                - link "[235]" [ref=e1132]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "235"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - superscript
                - link "[236]" [ref=e1133]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "236"
                    - StaticText "]"
            - paragraph
              - StaticText "While most Americans were proud of their nation's achievements in space exploration, only once during the late 1960s did the "
              - link "Gallup Poll" [ref=e716]
              - StaticText " indicate that a majority of Americans favored \"doing more\" in space as opposed to \"doing less\". By 1973, 59 percent of those polled favored cutting spending on space exploration. The Space Race had been won, and Cold War tensions were easing as the US and Soviet Union entered the era of "
              - link "détente" [ref=e717]
              - StaticText ". This was also a time when inflation was rising, which put pressure on the government to reduce spending. The space program was saved due to the perception that it was one of the few government programs that had achieved something great. Drastic cuts, warned "
              - link "Caspar Weinberger" [ref=e718]
              - StaticText ", the deputy director of the "
              - link "Office of Management and Budget" [ref=e719]
              - StaticText ", might send a signal that \"our best years are behind us\"."
              - superscript
                - link "[237]" [ref=e1134]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "237"
                    - StaticText "]"
            - paragraph
              - StaticText "After the Apollo 11 mission, officials from the Soviet Union said landing humans on the Moon was dangerous and unnecessary. At the time the Soviet Union was attempting to retrieve lunar samples robotically. The Soviets publicly denied there was a race to the Moon, and indicated they were not making an attempt."
              - superscript
                - link "[238]" [ref=e1135]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "238"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " "
              - link "Mstislav Keldysh" [ref=e720]
              - StaticText " said in July 1969, \"We are concentrating wholly on the creation of large satellite systems.\" It was revealed in 1989 that the Soviets had tried to send people to the Moon, but were unable due to technological difficulties."
              - superscript
                - link "[239]" [ref=e1136]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "239"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " The public's reaction in the Soviet Union was mixed. The Soviet government limited the release of information about the lunar landing, which affected the reaction. A portion of the populace did not give it any attention, and another portion was angered by it."

            - paragraph
              - StaticText "The descent stage of the LM "

              - StaticText " remains on the Moon. In 2009, the "

              - StaticText " (LRO) imaged the various Apollo landing sites on the surface of the Moon, for the first time with sufficient resolution to see the descent stages of the lunar modules, scientific instruments, and foot trails made by the astronauts."
              - superscript
                - link "[252]" [ref=e1156]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "252"
                    - StaticText "]"
            - paragraph
              - StaticText "The remains of the ascent stage are assumed to lie at an unknown location on the lunar surface. The ascent stage, "
              - StaticText "Eagle"
              - StaticText ", was not tracked after it was jettisoned. The lunar gravity field is sufficiently non-uniform to make low Moon orbits unstable after a short time, leading the orbiting object to impact the surface."
              - superscript
                - link "[253]" [ref=e1157]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["
                    - StaticText "253"
                    - StaticText "]"
              - StaticText " However, using a program developed by NASA, and high-resolution lunar gravity data, a paper was published, in 2021, indicating that "
              - StaticText "Eagle"
              - StaticText " might still be in orbit as late as 2020. Using the orbital elements published by NASA, a "
              - link "Monte Carlo method" [ref=e742]
              - StaticText " was used to generate parameter sets that bracket the uncertainties in these elements. All simulations, of the orbit, predicted that "
              - StaticText "Eagle"
              - StaticText " would never impact the lunar surface."
              - superscript
                - link "[254]" [ref=e1158]
                  - generic
                    - StaticText "["

            - paragraph
              - StaticText "The main repository for the Apollo Moon rocks is the "
              - link "Lunar Sample Laboratory Facility" [ref=e751]
              - StaticText " at the Lyndon B. Johnson Space Center in "
              - link "Houston" [ref=e752]
              - StaticText ", Texas. For safekeeping, there is also a smaller collection stored at "
              - link "White Sands Test Facility" [ref=e753]
              - StaticText " near "
              - link "Las Cruces, New Mexico" [ref=e754]
              - StaticText ". Most of the rocks are stored in nitrogen to keep them free of moisture. They are handled only indirectly, using special tools. Over 100 research laboratories worldwide conduct studies of the samples; approximately 500 samples are prepared and sent to investigators every year."

              - StaticText "mg and were enveloped in a clear acrylic button about as big as a "
